<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016ukTh1iNZ3BeR9cFoq6Eeb`</summary>

```text
64	\paragraph{Type A (SC $\geq$ $\sim$60\%): confirmation bias.} Eight conditions with SC $\geq 63\%$ (combined $N > 2{,}200$) produce no significant positive effects. Most yield $|\Delta| \leq 2.45$\,pp (nonsignificant), but Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 75.17\%$) shows a significant negative effect: $\Delta = -2.50$\,pp ($p = 0.011$), indicating that symbolic aggregation can hurt even when the model is accurate. Qwen2.5-14B on two other powered datasets (FOLIO $n = 204$, LogiQA $n = 200$) yields $|\Delta| \leq 0.50$\,pp. Even in-context learning with gold FOL examples (SC $= 74.37\%$, $\Delta = +0.50$\,pp, $n = 199$) fails to break the pattern: improving extraction quality through exemplars does not overcome confirmation bias when the model already produces consistent traces. Qwen3-14B (SC $= 84.80\%$) represents a generational capability improvement yet produces $\Delta = -0.49$\,pp ($p > 0.05$).
65	
66	\paragraph{Type C (SC $\leq$ $\sim$40\%): error amplification.} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\%$) shows $\Delta = -2.83$\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \emph{significantly hurts} performance. Constraints extracted from predominantly incorrect traces encode wrong reasoning as formal rules, and the solver amplifies these errors rather than correcting them.
```

</details>

A second seed shows a consistent direction at $\\Delta = +2.94$\\,pp ($p = 0.070$, marginal); the third seed shows a null result ($\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp, $p = 1.0$; see \\S\\ref{sec:replication}). No seed reaches significance at $\\alpha = 0.05$. Mistral-7B on LogiQA (SC $= 51.00\\%$), despite falling in the same SC range, yields only $\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp ($p = 1.0$). Section~\\ref{sec:failure-modes} attributes this to domain formalizability.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Type A (SC $\\geq$ $\\sim$60\\%): confirmation bias.} Eight conditions with SC $\\geq 63\\%$ (combined $N > 2{,}200$) produce no significant positive effects. Most yield $|\\Delta| \\leq 2.45$\\,pp (nonsignificant), but Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 75.17\\%$) shows a significant negative effect: $\\Delta = -2.50$\\,pp ($p = 0.011$), indicating that symbolic aggregation can hurt even when the model is accurate. Qwen2.5-14B on two other powered datasets (FOLIO $n = 204$, LogiQA $n = 200$) yields $|\\Delta| \\leq 0.50$\\,pp. Even in-context learning with gold FOL examples (SC $= 74.37\\%$, $\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp, $n = 199$) fails to break the pattern: improving extraction quality through exemplars does not overcome confirmation bias when the model already produces consistent traces. Qwen3-14B (SC $= 84.80\\%$) represents a generational capability improvement yet produces $\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp ($p > 0.05$).",

          " Recent work has examined CJT violations in LLM ensembles \\citep{lefort2024condorcet_llm}, but no prior study connects this theoretical framework to symbolic constraint extraction.",
          " The distinction matters: symbolic constraints appear to introduce a qualitatively different information source (formal logic rather than natural language votes), yet our analysis shows that the extraction step collapses this apparent independence back into the shared model distribution.",
          " ",
          "-We evaluate SICA across six models (7B to 32B parameters), three reasoning domains (FOLIO \\citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \\citep{liu2020logiqa}), and more than 2,400 evaluation instances with three-seed replication on the primary condition (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).",
          "+We evaluate SICA across six models (7B to 32B parameters), three reasoning domains (FOLIO \\citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \\citep{liu2020logiqa}), and more than 3,000 evaluation instances with three-seed replication on the primary condition (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).",
          " Our evaluation spans instruction-tuned and reasoning-specialized model variants to ensure the findings are not artifacts of a single model family.",
          " The central finding is the independence bottleneck itself; as a secondary observation, we find positive directional trends in a narrow region around 50--55\\% SC accuracy in one model-dataset condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO, 2 of 3 seeds with $p < 0.10$, none reaching $p < 0.05$; best $+3.43$\\,pp).",
          " Outside it, three distinct failure modes emerge (\\S\\ref{sec:failure-modes})."

    "oldString": "and over 2,400 evaluation instances with three-seed replication",
    "newString": "and over 3,000 evaluation instances with three-seed replication",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe evaluated Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) across six models spanning three architecture families across three reasoning domains (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA), and over 2,400 evaluation instances with three-seed replication on the primary condition. Grounded in the Condorcet Jury Theorem, our analysis explains why Self-Consistency majority voting saturates and why symbolic constraint aggregation fails to break this plateau under same-model self-extraction.\n\nThe central finding is the independence bottleneck. Same-model traces produce Fleiss' $\\kappa$ values of $0.21$--$0.82$ among instruction-tuned models (0.91 for thinking mode), and the effective sample size compresses to Eff-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ at $K = 12$. Self-extracted FOL constraints inherit this correlation structure. We observe a narrow favorable region around 50--55\\% SC accuracy in one model-dataset condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO): Mistral-7B on FOLIO shows a positive directional trend of $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$, 2 of 3 seeds marginal at $p < 0.10$, none reaching $p < 0.05$; 3-seed mean $+2.12$\\,pp). Outside this zone, confirmation bias renders constraints non-discriminative (Type~A) or error amplification actively degrades accuracy by $-2.83$\\,pp (Type~C).\n\nOracle controls confirm that the aggregation mechanism is sound: gold FOL annotations yield $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling. Nine remediation strategies spanning six intervention dimensions all fail to widen the effective zone. The uniform failure confirms the bottleneck is structural rather than an engineering artifact. Sensitivity analysis reinforces this conclusion: $K = 12$ is the only setting with $\\Delta > 2$\\,pp among five $K$ values, and only 1 of 4 temperature settings ($T = 0.7$) produces a positive effect; both peaks coincide with a relatively low SC baseline.\n\nThe multi-candidate rate $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost deployment diagnostic for predicting when symbolic aggregation can help. Three paths forward trade simplicity for independence as CJT requires: trained extractors, external provers, and multi-model pipelines.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} Our evaluation covers same-model self-extraction and cross-model extraction in both directions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}), but we do not test trained FOL extractors or external solvers with verified grounding, which may overcome the independence bottleneck. The observed favorable region is narrow (SC $\\approx$ 50--55\\%), observed in only one model-dataset condition, limiting practical applicability. The optimal $K$ and temperature settings cannot be determined without labeled data, as both sensitivity analyses show the best configuration coincides with the lowest SC baseline rather than following a predictable trend. Our evaluation covers formal reasoning benchmarks (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA) but lacks open-domain validation where formalizability is lower and failure modes may differ. Finally, three-seed replication applies only to the primary condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO), where one of three seeds yields a null result, indicating that even the strongest positive finding is sensitive to sampling variance.\n",

_source line 6856 · 2026-05-19T11:03:44.317Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011aiDV72DVeEX8uz1KH1yVH`</summary>

```text
1	Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over multiple chain-of-thought traces is the dominant test-time aggregation strategy for large language models (LLMs), but accuracy gains saturate as sample count grows. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) explains this plateau: same-model traces are correlated voters, so additional samples carry diminishing independent information. Symbolic constraints expressed in first-order logic (FOL) appear to offer a way forward, since formal validity is independent of the generative model. Yet when constraints are extracted by the same model that produces the traces, they inherit the traces' correlation structure. We term this the \emph{independence bottleneck}. We study this bottleneck through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts FOL constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT. Evaluating six models (7B to 32B parameters) across three reasoning domains with over 2,400 instances and three-seed replication, we find a regime-dependent pattern: positive directional trends ($p < 0.10$ in 2 of 3 seeds, none reaching $p < 0.05$; best seed $+3.43$\,pp, 3-seed mean $+2.12$\,pp) occur only in a narrow band around 50 to 55\% SC baseline on a highly formalizable domain (one model, one dataset). Outside this zone, constraints either echo the majority (confirmation bias) or amplify errors. Cross-model entropy decomposition reveals the mechanism: all SICA effects concentrate in high-entropy problems, but the direction depends on the accuracy regime. Oracle controls with gold FOL annotations confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound, yet self-extraction captures only 24\% of the oracle ceiling. Nine remediation strategies spanning six intervention dimensions all fail to widen the effective zone. The bottleneck is structural: same-model self-extraction cannot supply the independent signal that CJT requires for aggregation to surpass majority voting.
2	
```

62	
63	In the favorable region (SC $\sim$50--55\%), Mistral-7B on FOLIO shows positive directional trends. Seed~42 achieves $\Delta = +3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.092$, marginal). A second seed shows a consistent direction at $\Delta = +2.94$\,pp ($p = 0.070$, marginal); the third seed shows a null result ($\Delta = 0.00$\,pp, $p = 1.0$; see \S\ref{sec:replication}). No seed reaches significance at $\alpha = 0.05$. Mistral-7B on LogiQA (SC $= 51.00\%$), despite falling in the same SC range, yields only $\Delta = +0.50$\,pp ($p = 1.0$). Section~\ref{sec:failure-modes} attributes this to domain formalizability.
64	
65	\paragraph{Type A (SC $\geq$ $\sim$60\%): confirmation bias.} Nine conditions with SC $\geq 63\%$ (combined $N > 2{,}800$) produce no significant positive effects. Most yield $|\Delta| \leq 2.45$\,pp (nonsignificant), but Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 75.17\%$) shows a significant negative effect: $\Delta = -2.50$\,pp ($p = 0.011$), indicating that symbolic aggregation can hurt even when the model is accurate. Qwen2.5-14B on two other powered datasets (FOLIO $n = 204$, LogiQA $n = 200$) yields $|\Delta| \leq 0.50$\,pp. Even in-context learning with gold FOL examples (SC $= 74.37\%$, $\Delta = +0.50$\,pp, $n = 199$) fails to break the pattern: improving extraction quality through exemplars does not overcome confirmation bias when the model already produces consistent traces. Qwen3-14B (SC $= 84.80\%$) represents a generational capability improvement yet produces $\Delta = -0.49$\,pp ($p > 0.05$).
66	
67	\paragraph{Type C (SC $\leq$ $\sim$40\%): error amplification.} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\%$) shows $\Delta = -2.83$\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \emph{significantly hurts} performance. Constraints extracted from predominantly incorrect traces encode wrong reasoning as formal rules, and the solver amplifies these errors rather than correcting them.
68	
69	\paragraph{Degenerate cases.} When $C_0 < 5\%$ (QwQ-32B on AIME 2024, Qwen2.5-14B on GSM8K), nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction. These probes confirm the $C_0$ diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:method}).
70	
71	Figure~\ref{fig:regime-scatter} visualizes the regime dependence. The favorable region is narrow and observed only for Mistral-7B on FOLIO: the upper boundary is sharp (LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO at SC $= 63.24\%$ already shows $\Delta = -2.45$\,pp), and below $\sim$40\% SC, constraints transition from noisy to actively harmful. The oracle rows (bottom of Table~\ref{tab:main_results}) confirm that gold constraints yield $\Delta = +14.22$\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$), establishing that the aggregation mechanism is sound when constraints are discriminative (\S\ref{sec:oracle}).
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011FS8nhEidLpHwuTmGs9ZpP`</summary>

```text
89	
90	
91	\subsection{Three-Seed Replication}
92	\label{sec:replication}
93	
94	The favorable-region result rests on Mistral-7B FOLIO performance, so we replicate with three random seeds to assess stability. All three runs use identical hyperparameters ($K = 12$, $T = 0.7$, $T_{\text{ext}} = 0.3$) on the full FOLIO evaluation set ($n = 204$).
95	
96	Seed~42 produces $\Delta = +3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.092$, SC $= 55.88\%$). Seed~123 shows a consistent direction at $\Delta = +2.94$\,pp ($p = 0.070$, SC $= 54.41\%$). Seed~456 shows a null result ($\Delta = 0.00$\,pp, $p = 1.0$, SC $= 55.88\%$). No seed reaches statistical significance at $\alpha = 0.05$; two of three show marginal positive trends ($p < 0.10$). The mean effect across seeds is $\Delta = +2.12$\,pp, consistent with the regime-boundary fragility that motivates our independence analysis.
97	
98	The variance is informative. Seeds~42 and~456 share identical SC baselines (55.88\%) yet produce different SICA outcomes ($+3.43$ vs.\ $0.00$\,pp), indicating that SC level alone does not determine the effect; seed-level constraint quality also matters. Sampling noise at the regime boundary can push individual seeds across the transition, which makes the effect fragile by design rather than by accident. This seed-level sensitivity motivates the entropy decomposition in \S\ref{sec:entropy}, where per-problem trace agreement reveals that the aggregate $\Delta$ is driven entirely by the high-entropy subpopulation: seed~42 achieves $+12.8$\,pp on high-entropy problems while seed~456 shows $-1.3$\,pp on the same partition.
99	
100	
101	\subsection{Failure Mode Analysis}
102	\label{sec:failure-modes}
103	
104	The regime-dependent pattern in Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reflects three distinct failure modes of self-extracted symbolic constraints.
105	
106	\paragraph{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\geq$ $\sim$60\%).} Strong models produce consistent reasoning traces, and constraints extracted from those traces mirror the answer distribution. When most constraints support the same candidate, MAX-SAT excludes few clauses ($|\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}| \approx 0$), the penalty term in Eq.~\ref{eq:score} vanishes, and the scoring function degenerates to weighted vote counting (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). SICA and SC then produce identical predictions. The ablation in \S\ref{sec:ablation} confirms this empirically: on Qwen2.5-14B (FOLIO + ProofWriter, $n = 804$), MAX-SAT and count-only baselines agree on 803 of 804 predictions.
107	
108	\paragraph{Type B: non-formalizable domains.} LogiQA problems require pragmatic and contextual reasoning that resists clean FOL formalization. Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA achieves an extraction rate of 86.4\% (fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint), but the resulting constraints have a contradiction rate of only 0.8\%: extracted formulas rarely conflict across traces, leaving MAX-SAT with no logical tension to exploit. Mistral-7B on LogiQA sits in the same SC range (51.00\%) yet produces $\Delta = +0.50$\,pp ($p = 1.0$); Qwen2.5-14B on the same domain yields $\Delta = -0.50$\,pp ($p = 1.0$). Neither model benefits, confirming that the right accuracy regime is necessary but not sufficient when the domain resists formalization.
```

</details>

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\subsection{Three-Seed Replication}\n\\label{sec:replication}\n\nThe favorable-region result rests on Mistral-7B FOLIO performance, so we replicate with three random seeds to assess stability. All three runs use identical hyperparameters ($K = 12$, $T = 0.7$, $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$) on the full FOLIO evaluation set ($n = 204$).\n\nSeed~42 produces $\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$, SC $= 55.88\\%$). Seed~123 shows a consistent direction at $\\Delta = +2.94$\\,pp ($p = 0.070$, SC $= 54.41\\%$). Seed~456 shows a null result ($\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp, $p = 1.0$, SC $= 55.88\\%$). No seed reaches statistical significance at $\\alpha = 0.05$; two of three show marginal positive trends ($p < 0.10$). The mean effect across seeds is $\\Delta = +2.12$\\,pp, consistent with the regime-boundary fragility that motivates our independence analysis.\n\nThe variance is informative. Seeds~42 and~456 share identical SC baselines (55.88\\%) yet produce different SICA outcomes ($+3.43$ vs.\\ $0.00$\\,pp), indicating that SC level alone does not determine the effect; seed-level constraint quality also matters. Sampling noise at the regime boundary can push individual seeds across the transition, which makes the effect fragile by design rather than by accident. This seed-level sensitivity motivates the entropy decomposition in \\S\\ref{sec:entropy}, where per-problem trace agreement reveals that the aggregate $\\Delta$ is driven entirely by the high-entropy subpopulation: seed~42 achieves $+12.8$\\,pp on high-entropy problems while seed~456 shows $-1.3$\\,pp on the same partition.",
  "new_string": "\\subsection{Four-Seed Replication}\n\\label{sec:replication}\n\nThe favorable-region result rests on Mistral-7B FOLIO performance, so we replicate with four random seeds to assess stability. All four runs use identical hyperparameters ($K = 12$, $T = 0.7$, $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$) on the full FOLIO evaluation set ($n = 204$).\n\nSeed~42 produces $\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$, SC $= 55.88\\%$). Seed~123 shows a consistent direction at $\\Delta = +2.94$\\,pp ($p = 0.070$, SC $= 54.41\\%$). Seed~456 shows a null result ($\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp, $p = 1.0$, SC $= 55.88\\%$). Seed~789 shows a negative result ($\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp, $p = 1.0$, SC $= 54.41\\%$). No seed reaches statistical significance at $\\alpha = 0.05$; 2 of 4 show marginal positive trends ($p < 0.10$). The mean effect across four seeds is $\\Delta = +1.47$\\,pp, with effects ranging from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp. Cross-seed variance is high, confirming that even the most favorable condition produces unreliable improvements.\n\nThe variance is informative. Seeds~42 and~456 share identical SC baselines (55.88\\%) yet produce different SICA outcomes ($+3.43$ vs.\\ $0.00$\\,pp), indicating that SC level alone does not determine the effect; seed-level constraint quality also matters. Sampling noise at the regime boundary can push individual seeds across the transition, which makes the effect fragile by design rather than by accident. This seed-level sensitivity motivates the entropy decomposition in \\S\\ref{sec:entropy}, where per-problem trace agreement reveals that the aggregate $\\Delta$ is driven entirely by the high-entropy subpopulation: seed~42 achieves $+12.8$\\,pp on high-entropy problems while seed~456 shows $-1.3$\\,pp on the same partition."
}

    "oldString": "and three-seed replication, we find a regime-dependent pattern: positive directional trends ($p < 0.10$ in 2 of 3 seeds, none reaching $p < 0.05$; best seed $+3.43$\\,pp, 3-seed mean $+2.12$\\,pp)",
    "newString": "and four-seed replication, we find a regime-dependent pattern: positive directional trends ($p < 0.10$ in 2 of 4 seeds, none reaching $p < 0.05$; best seed $+3.43$\\,pp, 4-seed mean $+1.47$\\,pp)",
    "originalFile": "Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over multiple chain-of-thought traces is the dominant test-time aggregation strategy for large language models (LLMs), but accuracy gains saturate as sample count grows. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) explains this plateau: same-model traces are correlated voters, so additional samples carry diminishing independent information. Symbolic constraints expressed in first-order logic (FOL) appear to offer a way forward, since formal validity is independent of the generative model. Yet when constraints are extracted by the same model that produces the traces, they inherit the traces' correlation structure. We term this the \\emph{independence bottleneck}. We study this bottleneck through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts FOL constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT. Evaluating six models (7B to 32B parameters) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 instances and three-seed replication, we find a regime-dependent pattern: positive directional trends ($p < 0.10$ in 2 of 3 seeds, none reaching $p < 0.05$; best seed $+3.43$\\,pp, 3-seed mean $+2.12$\\,pp) occur only in a narrow band around 50 to 55\\% SC baseline on a highly formalizable domain (one model, one dataset). Outside this zone, constraints either echo the majority (confirmation bias) or amplify errors. Cross-model entropy decomposition reveals the mechanism: all SICA effects concentrate in high-entropy problems, but the direction depends on the accuracy regime. Oracle controls with gold FOL annotations confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound, yet self-extraction captures only 24\\% of the oracle ceiling. Nine remediation strategies spanning six intervention dimensions all fail to widen the effective zone. The bottleneck is structural: same-model self-extraction cannot supply the independent signal that CJT requires for aggregation to surpass majority voting.\n",

Yet when constraints are extracted by the same model that produces the traces, they inherit the traces' correlation structure. We term this the \\emph{independence bottleneck}. We study this bottleneck through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts FOL constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT. Evaluating six models (7B to 32B parameters) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 instances and three-seed replication, we find a regime-dependent pattern: positive directional trends ($p < 0.10$ in 2 of 3 seeds, none reaching $p < 0.05$; best seed $+3.43$\\,pp, 3-seed mean $+2.12$\\,pp) occur only in a narrow band around 50 to 55\\% SC baseline on a highly formalizable domain (one model, one dataset). Outside this zone, constraints either echo the majority (confirmation bias) or amplify errors. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) explains this plateau: same-model traces are correlated voters, so additional samples carry diminishing independent information.           " Recent work has examined CJT violations in LLM ensembles \\citep{lefort2024condorcet_llm}, but no prior study connects this theoretical framework to symbolic constraint extraction.",

          " ",
          "-We evaluate SICA across six models (7B to 32B parameters), three reasoning domains (FOLIO \\citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \\citep{liu2020logiqa}), and more than 3,000 evaluation instances with three-seed replication on the primary condition (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).",
          "+We evaluate SICA across six models (7B to 32B parameters), three reasoning domains (FOLIO \\citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \\citep{liu2020logiqa}), and more than 3,000 evaluation instances with four-seed replication on the primary condition (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).",
          " Our evaluation spans instruction-tuned and reasoning-specialized model variants to ensure the findings are not artifacts of a single model family.",
          " The central finding is the independence bottleneck itself; as a secondary observation, we find positive directional trends in a narrow region around 50--55\\% SC accuracy in one model-dataset condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO, 2 of 3 seeds with $p < 0.10$, none reaching $p < 0.05$; best $+3.43$\\,pp).",
          " Outside it, three distinct failure modes emerge (\\S\\ref{sec:failure-modes})."

          "+The central finding is the independence bottleneck itself; as a secondary observation, we find positive directional trends in a narrow region around 50--55\\% SC accuracy in one model-dataset condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO, 2 of 4 seeds with $p < 0.10$, none reaching $p < 0.05$; best $+3.43$\\,pp).",
          " Outside it, three distinct failure modes emerge (\\S\\ref{sec:failure-modes}).",
          " In Type~A conditions (SC $\\geq$ 60\\%), constraints exhibit confirmation bias and merely echo the majority answer; SICA provides no significant positive effect across nine conditions ($N > 2{,}800$ combined), and one condition shows significant degradation ($-2.50$\\,pp, $p = 0.011$).",
          " In Type~C conditions (SC $\\leq$ 40\\%), predominantly incorrect traces produce noisy constraints that actively degrade accuracy ($-2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$)."
        ]
      }

```text
62	\begin{enumerate}
63	\item \textbf{Independence Bottleneck.} We formalize the SC plateau through the Condorcet Jury Theorem: same-model traces yield Fleiss' $\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ (Eff-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ for $K = 12$), quantifying why additional traces provide diminishing returns. Trace correlation affects symbolic aggregation through two channels: it directly limits voting diversity (explaining the SC plateau) and indirectly produces homogeneous constraints that cause MAX-SAT to degenerate to vote counting (explaining SICA's failure to surpass SC). Oracle controls disambiguate these channels (\S\ref{sec:condorcet}, \S\ref{sec:effective-k}).
64	
65	\item \textbf{Oracle-Extraction Gap.} Oracle constraints yield $+14.22$\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 0.001$), proving the aggregation mechanism is sound. Self-extraction captures only 24\% of this ceiling. The failure localizes to extraction quality, not the symbolic approach (\S\ref{sec:oracle}).
66	
67	\item \textbf{Regime-Dependent Effect.} Positive directional trends occur in a narrow accuracy band ($\sim$50--55\% SC) in one model on one highly formalizable dataset (2 of 3 seeds marginal at $p < 0.10$, none reaching $p < 0.05$; best $+3.43$\,pp, 3-seed mean $+2.12$\,pp). Above this range, confirmation bias renders constraints non-discriminative. Below it, error amplification degrades accuracy. Three failure modes (Type~A, B, C) explain the regime boundaries (\S\ref{sec:main-results}, \S\ref{sec:failure-modes}).
68	
69	\item \textbf{Structural Bottleneck Diagnosis.} Nine remediation strategies spanning prompting, architecture, capability, verification, diversity, and filtering all fail to widen the effective zone, confirming the independence bottleneck is inherent to same-model self-extraction. $K$/temperature sensitivity analysis and cross-model entropy decomposition provide practitioners with deployment diagnostics for predicting when symbolic aggregation can and cannot help (\S\ref{sec:remediation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
70	\end{enumerate}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01ALxp8QdSc9Hz4LBB2BKzbE`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We evaluated Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) across six models spanning three architecture families across three reasoning domains (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA), and over 3,000 evaluation instances with three-seed replication on the primary condition. Grounded in the Condorcet Jury Theorem, our analysis explains why Self-Consistency majority voting saturates and why symbolic constraint aggregation fails to break this plateau under same-model self-extraction.
4	
5	The central finding is the independence bottleneck. Same-model traces produce Fleiss' $\kappa$ values of $0.21$--$0.82$ among instruction-tuned models (0.91 for thinking mode), and the effective sample size compresses to Eff-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ at $K = 12$. Self-extracted FOL constraints inherit this correlation structure. We observe a narrow favorable region around 50--55\% SC accuracy in one model-dataset condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO): Mistral-7B on FOLIO shows a positive directional trend of $+3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.092$, 2 of 3 seeds marginal at $p < 0.10$, none reaching $p < 0.05$; 3-seed mean $+2.12$\,pp). Outside this zone, confirmation bias renders constraints non-discriminative (Type~A) or error amplification actively degrades accuracy by $-2.83$\,pp (Type~C).
6	
7	Oracle controls confirm that the aggregation mechanism is sound: gold FOL annotations yield $+14.22$\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only 24\% of this ceiling. Nine remediation strategies spanning six intervention dimensions all fail to widen the effective zone. The uniform failure confirms the bottleneck is structural rather than an engineering artifact. Sensitivity analysis reinforces this conclusion: $K = 12$ is the only setting with $\Delta > 2$\,pp among five $K$ values, and only 1 of 4 temperature settings ($T = 0.7$) produces a positive effect; both peaks coincide with a relatively low SC baseline.
8	
9	The multi-candidate rate $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost deployment diagnostic for predicting when symbolic aggregation can help. Three paths forward trade simplicity for independence as CJT requires: trained extractors, external provers, and multi-model pipelines.
10	
11	\paragraph{Limitations.} Our evaluation covers same-model self-extraction and cross-model extraction in both directions (\S\ref{sec:remediation}), but we do not test trained FOL extractors or external solvers with verified grounding, which may overcome the independence bottleneck. The observed favorable region is narrow (SC $\approx$ 50--55\%), observed in only one model-dataset condition, limiting practical applicability. The optimal $K$ and temperature settings cannot be determined without labeled data, as both sensitivity analyses show the best configuration coincides with the lowest SC baseline rather than following a predictable trend. Our evaluation covers formal reasoning benchmarks (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA) but lacks open-domain validation where formalizability is lower and failure modes may differ. Finally, three-seed replication applies only to the primary condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO), where one of three seeds yields a null result, indicating that even the strongest positive finding is sensitive to sampling variance.
12	
```

</details>

      "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe evaluated Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) across six models spanning three architecture families across three reasoning domains (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA), and over 3,000 evaluation instances with three-seed replication on the primary condition. Grounded in the Condorcet Jury Theorem, our analysis explains why Self-Consistency majority voting saturates and why symbolic constraint aggregation fails to break this plateau under same-model self-extraction.\n\nThe central finding is the independence bottleneck. Same-model traces produce Fleiss' $\\kappa$ values of $0.21$--$0.82$ among instruction-tuned models (0.91 for thinking mode), and the effective sample size compresses to Eff-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ at $K = 12$. Self-extracted FOL constraints inherit this correlation structure. We observe a narrow favorable region around 50--55\\% SC accuracy in one model-dataset condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO): Mistral-7B on FOLIO shows a positive directional trend of $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$, 2 of 3 seeds marginal at $p < 0.10$, none reaching $p < 0.05$; 3-seed mean $+2.12$\\,pp). Outside this zone, confirmation bias renders constraints non-discriminative (Type~A) or error amplification actively degrades accuracy by $-2.83$\\,pp (Type~C).\n\nOracle controls confirm that the aggregation mechanism is sound: gold FOL annotations yield $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling. Nine remediation strategies spanning six intervention dimensions all fail to widen the effective zone. The uniform failure confirms the bottleneck is structural rather than an engineering artifact. Sensitivity analysis reinforces this conclusion: $K = 12$ is the only setting with $\\Delta > 2$\\,pp among five $K$ values, and only 1 of 4 temperature settings ($T = 0.7$) produces a positive effect; both peaks coincide with a relatively low SC baseline.\n\nThe multi-candidate rate $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost deployment diagnostic for predicting when symbolic aggregation can help. Three paths forward trade simplicity for independence as CJT requires: trained extractors, external provers, and multi-model pipelines.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} Our evaluation covers same-model self-extraction and cross-model extraction in both directions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}), but we do not test trained FOL extractors or external solvers with verified grounding, which may overcome the independence bottleneck. The observed favorable region is narrow (SC $\\approx$ 50--55\\%), observed in only one model-dataset condition, limiting practical applicability. The optimal $K$ and temperature settings cannot be determined without labeled data, as both sensitivity analyses show the best configuration coincides with the lowest SC baseline rather than following a predictable trend. Our evaluation covers formal reasoning benchmarks (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA) but lacks open-domain validation where formalizability is lower and failure modes may differ. Finally, three-seed replication applies only to the primary condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO), where one of three seeds yields a null result, indicating that even the strongest positive finding is sensitive to sampling variance.\n",
      "numLines": 12,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 12
    }
  },

}

    "oldString": "Finally, three-seed replication applies only to the primary condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO), where one of three seeds yields a null result, indicating that even the strongest positive finding is sensitive to sampling variance.",
    "newString": "Finally, four-seed replication on the primary condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO) shows effects ranging from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp, with 2 of 4 seeds yielding null or negative results, indicating that even the most favorable condition produces unreliable improvements.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe evaluated Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) across six models spanning three architecture families across three reasoning domains (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA), and over 3,000 evaluation instances with four-seed replication on the primary condition. Grounded in the Condorcet Jury Theorem, our analysis explains why Self-Consistency majority voting saturates and why symbolic constraint aggregation fails to break this plateau under same-model self-extraction.\n\nThe central finding is the independence bottleneck. Same-model traces produce Fleiss' $\\kappa$ values of $0.21$--$0.82$ among instruction-tuned models (0.91 for thinking mode), and the effective sample size compresses to Eff-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ at $K = 12$. Self-extracted FOL constraints inherit this correlation structure. We observe a narrow favorable region around 50--55\\% SC accuracy in one model-dataset condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO): Mistral-7B on FOLIO shows a positive directional trend of $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$, 2 of 4 seeds marginal at $p < 0.10$, none reaching $p < 0.05$; 4-seed mean $+1.47$\\,pp). Outside this zone, confirmation bias renders constraints non-discriminative (Type~A) or error amplification actively degrades accuracy by $-2.83$\\,pp (Type~C).\n\nOracle controls confirm that the aggregation mechanism is sound: gold FOL annotations yield $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling. Nine remediation strategies spanning six intervention dimensions all fail to widen the effective zone. The uniform failure confirms the bottleneck is structural rather than an engineering artifact. Sensitivity analysis reinforces this conclusion: $K = 12$ is the only setting with $\\Delta > 2$\\,pp among five $K$ values, and only 1 of 4 temperature settings ($T = 0.7$) produces a positive effect; both peaks coincide with a relatively low SC baseline.\n\nThe multi-candidate rate $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost deployment diagnostic for predicting when symbolic aggregation can help. Three paths forward trade simplicity for independence as CJT requires: trained extractors, external provers, and multi-model pipelines.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} Our evaluation covers same-model self-extraction and cross-model extraction in both directions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}), but we do not test trained FOL extractors or external solvers with verified grounding, which may overcome the independence bottleneck. The observed favorable region is narrow (SC $\\approx$ 50--55\\%), observed in only one model-dataset condition, limiting practical applicability. The optimal $K$ and temperature settings cannot be determined without labeled data, as both sensitivity analyses show the best configuration coincides with the lowest SC baseline rather than following a predictable trend. Our evaluation covers formal reasoning benchmarks (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA) but lacks open-domain validation where formalizability is lower and failure modes may differ. Finally, three-seed replication applies only to the primary condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO), where one of three seeds yields a null result, indicating that even the strongest positive finding is sensitive to sampling variance.\n",

### Tool result

_source line 6933 · 2026-05-19T11:16:02.130Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013XVDCE3oX1bEbGegf7aF9t`</summary>

```text
28	These systems trade the simplicity of self-extraction for architectures that introduce genuinely independent signal, exactly the property that CJT requires \citep{ladha1992correlated}.
29	
30	\paragraph{Statistical evidence.} No positive SICA effect reaches statistical significance at $\alpha = 0.05$. The three-seed replication of Mistral-7B on FOLIO yields $p = 0.092$, $0.070$, and $1.0$; two of three seeds show marginal positive trends ($p < 0.10$) but none survives any correction for multiple comparisons. We report these as directional evidence, not significance claims. In contrast, the negative effects are significant: Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($\Delta = -2.83$\,pp, $p = 0.014$) and Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter ($\Delta = -2.50$\,pp, $p = 0.011$) both reach $p < 0.05$, confirming that error amplification in the Type~C regime is a reliable phenomenon. Oracle controls remain highly significant ($p < 0.001$ on two datasets). The converging evidence for the independence bottleneck does not rest on any single $p$-value but on the pattern across analyses: consistent positive direction in 2/3 seeds, cross-model entropy decomposition showing regime-dependent mechanism (\S\ref{sec:entropy}), discriminative rate CI of [7.6, 8.8]\%, and the oracle-extraction gap.
31	
32	For practitioners, $C_0$ (multi-candidate rate) combined with SC accuracy forms a zero-cost deployment diagnostic before any constraint extraction is attempted.
33	When $C_0 < 5\%$, SICA reduces to SC by construction (degenerate).
34	When SC exceeds ${\sim}60\%$, confirmation bias renders constraints non-discriminative (Type~A).
35	When SC falls below ${\sim}40\%$, error amplification makes constraints actively harmful (Type~C).
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nThe oracle experiments provide a causal identification of the failure source.\nHolding all pipeline components fixed and varying only the constraint source, gold annotations yield $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$; \\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\nThe best self-extraction result across all models and conditions captures only 24.1\\% of the FOLIO oracle ceiling.\nBecause the aggregation mechanism is identical in both conditions, the gap between oracle and self-extracted performance localizes entirely to extraction quality, not to solver limitations or the MAX-SAT formulation.\nThe aggregation mechanism is sound; the constraints are not.\n\nTrace correlation affects SICA through two channels. The direct channel limits voting diversity: the Condorcet Jury Theorem predicts that aggregation improves accuracy only when voters contribute independent information \\citep{condorcet1785, ladha1992correlated}, and Eff-$K < 2$ for many conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:independence}) confirms that same-model traces violate this requirement at $K = 12$. This channel explains the SC plateau. The indirect channel operates through constraint quality: correlated traces produce homogeneous constraints, yielding a discriminative rate of only 8.2\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:extraction-taxonomy}) and causing MAX-SAT to degenerate to weighted vote counting (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}). This channel explains why SICA fails to surpass SC. The oracle result disambiguates the two: gold constraints applied to the same correlated traces yield $+14.22$\\,pp, proving that channel (b) is the proximal bottleneck. Trace correlation is the root cause, but its effect on SICA is mediated primarily through constraint homogenization rather than through voting diversity alone.\n\nThe extraction step maps natural language traces to FOL through the same model distribution, so constraints inherit the correlation structure of the traces that produced them.\nEven symbolic representations, which appear to introduce a qualitatively different signal (formal logic rather than natural language votes), collapse back into the shared model distribution once the extraction is performed by the same model.\nThe independence bottleneck is not about symbolic reasoning as a formalism; it is about self-extraction being unable to introduce information that the model does not already encode in its traces.\n\nNine interventions spanning six dimensions all fail to widen the favorable region (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\nContrastive prompting reduces the non-discriminative rate from 8.2\\% to 6.0\\%, but the newly discriminative constraints still mirror model bias.\nCross-model extraction fails in both directions: a weaker model extracting from stronger traces yields 0\\% discriminative constraints, while stronger models (14B+) extracting from weaker traces (7B) produce accuracy within $\\pm$1\\,pp of self-extraction.\nOnly 1 of 4 temperature settings produces a positive SICA effect; $T = 0.3$, $0.5$, and $1.0$ all yield negative or null effects ($\\Delta \\in [-1.47, 0.00]$\\,pp).\nEven in-context learning with gold FOL examples ($\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp, n.s.) cannot break the confirmation bias pattern.\nThe consistent failure across orthogonal intervention strategies constitutes the strongest evidence that the bottleneck is structural, not an artifact of prompt design or solver configuration.\nIf any single dimension had succeeded, the bottleneck could be attributed to engineering; the uniform failure across all six implicates the self-extraction paradigm itself.\n\nPublished systems that report positive results on logical reasoning each bypass same-model self-extraction.\nDyLoS \\citep{xu2026dylos} composes specialized solvers, Aristotle \\citep{li2025aristotle} applies logic-complete decomposition, FoVer \\citep{wang2025fover} trains dedicated FOL verifiers, and Logic-LM \\citep{pan2023logiclm} routes problems to external provers.\nOur oracle result is consistent with their success: when constraints are discriminative (by whatever mechanism), symbolic aggregation works.\nThe critical difference is the source of constraints, not the aggregation procedure.\nThese systems trade the simplicity of self-extraction for architectures that introduce genuinely independent signal, exactly the property that CJT requires \\citep{ladha1992correlated}.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical evidence.} No positive SICA effect reaches statistical significance at $\\alpha = 0.05$. The three-seed replication of Mistral-7B on FOLIO yields $p = 0.092$, $0.070$, and $1.0$; two of three seeds show marginal positive trends ($p < 0.10$) but none survives any correction for multiple comparisons. We report these as directional evidence, not significance claims. In contrast, the negative effects are significant: Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($\\Delta = -2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$) and Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter ($\\Delta = -2.50$\\,pp, $p = 0.011$) both reach $p < 0.05$, confirming that error amplification in the Type~C regime is a reliable phenomenon. Oracle controls remain highly significant ($p < 0.001$ on two datasets). The converging evidence for the independence bottleneck does not rest on any single $p$-value but on the pattern across analyses: consistent positive direction in 2/3 seeds, cross-model entropy decomposition showing regime-dependent mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:entropy}), discriminative rate CI of [7.6, 8.8]\\%, and the oracle-extraction gap.\n\nFor practitioners, $C_0$ (multi-candidate rate) combined with SC accuracy forms a zero-cost deployment diagnostic before any constraint extraction is attempted.\nWhen $C_0 < 5\\%$, SICA reduces to SC by construction (degenerate).\nWhen SC exceeds ${\\sim}60\\%$, confirmation bias renders constraints non-discriminative (Type~A).\nWhen SC falls below ${\\sim}40\\%$, error amplification makes constraints actively harmful (Type~C).\nOnly a narrow band around 50--55\\% SC in one model-dataset condition shows positive effects, and even there, self-extraction captures barely a quarter of the oracle ceiling.\nThree paths forward trade simplicity for independence, as CJT predicts is necessary: trained extractors (data-driven), external provers (tool-augmented), and multi-model pipelines (ensemble-based).\nEach introduces a constraint source that is not a deterministic function of the reasoning model's own distribution.",

5	
6	\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}
7	\label{sec:effective-k}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\kappa$ and the resulting Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}) across 14 conditions with $K = 12$ traces, sorted by $\kappa$ descending. Among instruction-tuned models, $\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, and $K_{\text{eff}}$ spans $1.20\text{--}3.62$. Qwen3-14B in thinking mode reaches $\kappa = 0.91$ ($K_{\text{eff}} = 1.09$), compressing twelve traces to barely more than one independent sample. Among instruction-tuned models, Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA ($\kappa = 0.82$, $K_{\text{eff}} = 1.20$) shows the highest agreement. Mistral-7B on FOLIO produces moderate agreement ($\kappa \approx 0.50$ across three seeds, $K_{\text{eff}} \approx 1.85$), consistent with its position in the favorable region. Mistral-7B on ProofWriter has the lowest agreement ($\kappa = 0.21$, $K_{\text{eff}} = 3.62$), yet SICA hurts ($\Delta = -2.83$\,pp) because trace quality is too low (SC $= 39.33\%$). High $K_{\text{eff}}$ alone does not guarantee improvement; the model must also produce sufficiently accurate traces.
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\small
14	\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\kappa$ descending). $\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa (raw answer computation). $K_{\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline accuracy. $\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\,$p < 0.05$ (McNemar); positive $\Delta$ values do not reach significance. Qwen3 (think) uses thinking mode; all other models use instruction-tuned or non-thinking mode. Mistral-7B traces require answer normalization on 12--16\% of outputs; all Qwen models achieve 100\% valid outputs. multi-T $= T \in \{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\}$, 4 traces each.}

38	
39	Cross-seed comparison shows that Mistral-7B's three FOLIO seeds produce $\kappa$ in the range 0.49--0.51 ($K_{\text{eff}} \approx 1.8\text{--}1.9$), yet $\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $0.00$\,pp. The variation in SICA gain is not driven by trace independence but by seed-level constraint quality: which specific constraints the model extracts determines whether MAX-SAT reranking happens to correct or harm predictions. Multi-temperature sampling ($T \in \{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\}$) achieves $K_{\text{eff}} = 1.91$, comparable to single-temperature conditions ($K_{\text{eff}} \approx 1.84$), and produces $\Delta = 0.00$\,pp. Greater independence does not translate into SICA gain when constraints still mirror the model's answer distribution.
40	
41	Two conditions are jointly necessary for SICA to improve over SC: sufficient $K_{\text{eff}}$ (the Qwen conditions with $K_{\text{eff}} \leq 1.30$ never show positive $\Delta$) and sufficient SC accuracy (Mistral on ProofWriter with the highest $K_{\text{eff}} = 3.62$ but SC $= 39\%$ produces significant degradation ($p = 0.014$)). However, even when both conditions hold, seed-level sampling variance determines whether the constraint pool happens to contain discriminative information. The SC plateau remains explained by the correlation floor: beyond $K_{\text{eff}}$, additional traces are redundant.
42	
43	\subsection{Extraction Error Taxonomy}
44	\label{sec:extraction-taxonomy}
45	
46	Constraints must discriminate between candidate answers for MAX-SAT to produce a ranking different from majority vote. We measure discriminative power at two levels on Qwen2.5-14B across FOLIO and ProofWriter ($n = 804$). At the source-trace level, 39.2\% of total constraint weight originates from traces whose answer differs from the plurality, but only 12.1\% of individual constraints fall into this minority category. At the Z3 semantic level, 91.8\% of constraints [95\% CI: 91.2, 92.4] are technically discriminative, producing different satisfiability outcomes when evaluated against different candidate answers. Only 8.2\% are non-discriminative.
47	
48	The high discriminative rate does not translate into accuracy gains because discrimination mirrors the model's own answer distribution. Each trace produces constraints with its own variable names, so Z3 treats identically-intended propositions as unrelated symbols; among cross-answer constraint pairs, 56.4\% share zero Z3 variables. This variable isolation means constraints from different traces are trivially jointly satisfiable, and MAX-SAT retains nearly all of them regardless of source answer. Removing the 8.2\% non-discriminative constraints yields zero accuracy improvement. The discriminative majority does not provide independent signal; it reinforces the majority answer that SC would already select. This confirmation bias, not a lack of discrimination, explains why SICA degenerates to majority voting (\S\ref{sec:ablation}).
49

Multi-temperature sampling ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) achieves $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.91$, comparable to single-temperature conditions ($K_{\\text{eff}} \\approx 1.84$), and produces $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp. Greater independence does not translate into SICA gain when constraints still mirror the model's answer distribution.\n\nTwo conditions are jointly necessary for SICA to improve over SC: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (the Qwen conditions with $K_{\\text{eff}} \\leq 1.30$ never show positive $\\Delta$) and sufficient SC accuracy (Mistral on ProofWriter with the highest $K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ but SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation ($p = 0.014$)). However, even when both conditions hold, seed-level sampling variance determines whether the constraint pool happens to contain discriminative information. The SC plateau remains explained by the correlation floor: beyond $K_{\\text{eff}}$, additional traces are redundant.\n\n\\subsection{Extraction Error Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:extraction-taxonomy}\n\nConstraints must discriminate between candidate answers for MAX-SAT to produce a ranking different from majority vote. We measure discriminative power at two levels on Qwen2.5-14B across FOLIO and ProofWriter ($n = 804$). At the source-trace level, 39.2\\% of total constraint weight originates from traces whose answer differs from the plurality, but only 12.1\\% of individual constraints fall into this minority category. At the Z3 semantic level, 91.8\\% of constraints [95\\% CI: 91.2, 92.4] are technically discriminative, producing different satisfiability outcomes when evaluated against different candidate answers. Only 8.2\\% are non-discriminative.\n\nThe high discriminative rate does not translate into accuracy gains because discrimination mirrors the model's own answer distribution. Each trace produces constraints with its own variable names, so Z3 treats identically-intended propositions as unrelated symbols; among cross-answer constraint pairs, 56.4\\% share zero Z3 variables. This variable isolation means constraints from different traces are trivially jointly satisfiable, and MAX-SAT retains nearly all of them regardless of source answer. Removing the 8.2\\% non-discriminative constraints yields zero accuracy improvement. The discriminative majority does not provide independent signal; it reinforces the majority answer that SC would already select. This confirmation bias, not a lack of discrimination, explains why SICA degenerates to majority voting (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).\n",

      "startLine": 5,
      "totalLines": 114
    }

Filled markers: $n \geq 100$; open markers: $n < 100$. The favorable region (SC 50--55\%, shaded) contains the only positive directional trends, all from Mistral-7B on FOLIO (none reaching $p < 0.05$). Type~A (SC $\geq$ 60\%): all nonsignificant. Type~C (SC $\leq$ 40\%): SICA significantly hurts ($\Delta = -2.83$\,pp, $p = 0.014$).}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/experiments.tex:95:The favorable-region result rests on Mistral-7B FOLIO performance, so we replicate with four random seeds to assess stability. All four runs use identical hyperparameters ($K = 12$, $T = 0.7$, $T_{\text{ext}} = 0.3$) on the full FOLIO evaluation set ($n = 204$).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/experiments.tex:97:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:5:[Omitted long matching line]

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/discussion.tex:16:Nine interventions spanning six dimensions all fail to widen the favorable region (\S\ref{sec:remediation}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/appendix.tex:267:All three intervals contain zero. The narrowest interval (Qwen-14B, [$-$1.12, $+$1.62]) rules out practically meaningful gains ($>$2pp) even in the most favorable case. The widest interval (LLaMA-8B FOLIO, [$-$5.88, $+$0.98]) skews negative, indicating that SICA may slightly hurt accuracy in this condition. These results corroborate the McNemar tests and power analysis: no condition provides evidence that symbolic constraint aggregation improves over majority voting.

63	
64	In the favorable region (SC $\sim$50--55\%), Mistral-7B on FOLIO shows positive directional trends in 2 of 4 seeds. Seed~42 achieves $\Delta = +3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.092$, marginal) and seed~123 shows $\Delta = +2.94$\,pp ($p = 0.070$, marginal); the remaining seeds show null or negative results ($\Delta = 0.00$ and $-0.49$\,pp; see \S\ref{sec:replication}). No seed reaches significance at $\alpha = 0.05$; the 4-seed mean is $+1.47$\,pp. Mistral-7B on LogiQA (SC $= 51.00\%$), despite falling in the same SC range, yields only $\Delta = +0.50$\,pp ($p = 1.0$). Section~\ref{sec:failure-modes} attributes this to domain formalizability.
65	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EdYP65DmQBdugR6b3DT471`</summary>

```text
71	
72	Figure~\ref{fig:regime-scatter} visualizes the regime dependence. The favorable region is narrow and observed only for Mistral-7B on FOLIO: the upper boundary is sharp (LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO at SC $= 66.18\%$ already shows $\Delta = -4.41$\,pp), and below $\sim$40\% SC, constraints transition from noisy to actively harmful. The oracle rows (bottom of Table~\ref{tab:main_results}) confirm that gold constraints yield $\Delta = +14.22$\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$), establishing that the aggregation mechanism is sound when constraints are discriminative (\S\ref{sec:oracle}).
73	
74	\begin{figure}[t]
75	\centering
76	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{f2_regime_scatter}
77	\caption{SICA $\Delta$ (pp) vs.\ SC baseline accuracy across all conditions. Filled markers: $n \geq 100$; open markers: $n < 100$. The favorable region (SC 50--55\%, shaded) contains the only positive directional trends, all from Mistral-7B on FOLIO (none reaching $p < 0.05$). Type~A (SC $\geq$ 60\%): all nonsignificant. Type~C (SC $\leq$ 40\%): SICA significantly hurts ($\Delta = -2.83$\,pp, $p = 0.014$).}
78	\label{fig:regime-scatter}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011Xm9vmx22gBCFTBv8xaPeV`</summary>

```text
97	Seed~42 produces $\Delta = +3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.092$, SC $= 55.88\%$). Seed~123 shows a consistent direction at $\Delta = +2.94$\,pp ($p = 0.070$, SC $= 54.41\%$). Seed~456 shows a null result ($\Delta = 0.00$\,pp, $p = 1.0$, SC $= 55.88\%$). Seed~789 shows a negative result ($\Delta = -0.49$\,pp, $p = 1.0$, SC $= 54.41\%$). No seed reaches statistical significance at $\alpha = 0.05$; 2 of 4 show marginal positive trends ($p < 0.10$). The mean effect across four seeds is $\Delta = +1.47$\,pp, with effects ranging from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\,pp. Cross-seed variance is high, confirming that even the most favorable condition produces unreliable improvements.
98	
99	The variance is informative. Seeds~42 and~456 share identical SC baselines (55.88\%) yet produce different SICA outcomes ($+3.43$ vs.\ $0.00$\,pp), indicating that SC level alone does not determine the effect; seed-level constraint quality also matters. Sampling noise at the regime boundary can push individual seeds across the transition, which makes the effect fragile by design rather than by accident. This seed-level sensitivity motivates the entropy decomposition in \S\ref{sec:entropy}, where per-problem trace agreement reveals that the aggregate $\Delta$ is driven entirely by the high-entropy subpopulation: seed~42 achieves $+12.8$\,pp on high-entropy problems while seed~456 shows $-1.3$\,pp on the same partition.
```

          " The intermediate-SC result rests on Mistral-7B FOLIO performance, so we replicate with four random seeds to assess stability. All four runs use identical hyperparameters ($K = 12$, $T = 0.7$, $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$) on the full FOLIO evaluation set ($n = 204$).",
          " ",
          "-Seed~42 produces $\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$, SC $= 55.88\\%$). Seed~123 shows a consistent direction at $\\Delta = +2.94$\\,pp ($p = 0.070$, SC $= 54.41\\%$). Seed~456 shows a null result ($\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp, $p = 1.0$, SC $= 55.88\\%$). Seed~789 shows a negative result ($\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp, $p = 1.0$, SC $= 54.41\\%$). No seed reaches statistical significance at $\\alpha = 0.05$; 2 of 4 show marginal positive trends ($p < 0.10$). The mean effect across four seeds is $\\Delta = +1.47$\\,pp, with effects ranging from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp. Cross-seed variance is high, confirming that even the most favorable condition produces unreliable improvements.",
          "+Seed~42 produces $\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$, SC $= 55.88\\%$). Seed~123 shows a consistent direction at $\\Delta = +2.94$\\,pp ($p = 0.070$, SC $= 54.41\\%$).           " ",
          " The variance is informative. Sampling noise at the regime boundary can push individual seeds across the transition, which makes the effect fragile by design rather than by accident. This seed-level sensitivity motivates the entropy decomposition in \\S\\ref{sec:entropy}, where per-problem trace agreement reveals that the aggregate $\\Delta$ is driven entirely by the high-entropy subpopulation: seed~42 achieves $+12.8$\\,pp on high-entropy problems while seed~456 shows $-1.3$\\,pp on the same partition.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013Ytcjo9f1AoZop9s1Xv59s`</summary>

```text
9	Table~\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\kappa$ and the resulting Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions with $K = 12$ traces, sorted by $\kappa$ descending. Among instruction-tuned models, $\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, and $K_{\text{eff}}$ spans $1.20\text{--}3.62$. Qwen3-14B in thinking mode reaches $\kappa = 0.91$ ($K_{\text{eff}} = 1.09$), compressing twelve traces to barely more than one independent sample. Among instruction-tuned models, Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA ($\kappa = 0.82$, $K_{\text{eff}} = 1.20$) shows the highest agreement. Mistral-7B on FOLIO produces moderate agreement ($\kappa \approx 0.50$ across four seeds, $K_{\text{eff}} \approx 1.85$), consistent with its position in the favorable region. Mistral-7B on ProofWriter has the lowest agreement ($\kappa = 0.21$, $K_{\text{eff}} = 3.62$), yet SICA hurts ($\Delta = -2.83$\,pp) because trace quality is too low (SC $= 39.33\%$). High $K_{\text{eff}}$ alone does not guarantee improvement; the model must also produce sufficiently accurate traces.
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 7040 · 2026-05-19T11:19:53.453Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011vfZf8nEjc495iMbK6f8rq`</summary>

```text
54	
55	The apparent $\Delta$ peak at $K = 12$ is confounded with SC baseline. SICA absolute accuracy plateaus at 59.31\% (121/204) for $K \geq 12$: identical at $K = 12$ and $K = 20$. The $\Delta$ variation across these settings is driven primarily by SC fluctuations rather than by SICA improvement. $K = 12$ produces a relatively low SC (55.88\%), placing it in the favorable region; $K = 8$ (SC $= 58.33\%$) and $K = 20$ (SC $= 57.35\%$) have higher baselines, compressing $\Delta$ even though SICA accuracy does not change for $K \geq 12$. Additional traces beyond $K = 12$ provide no benefit to SICA absolute performance, consistent with the constraint quality bottleneck: more traces cannot overcome homogeneous extraction. The $K = 12$ $\Delta$ captures only 24.1\% of the oracle ceiling ($+14.22$\,pp), and the best $K$ depends on the model-dataset condition and cannot be determined without labeled data.
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 7046 · 2026-05-19T11:20:00.821Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KkmoYgGozw8YpT8Kcv8GZX`</summary>

```text
68	
69	The parallel between $K$ and $T$ sensitivity points to a common underlying variable. In both sweeps, the setting that produces the largest SICA gain also produces a relatively low SC baseline (55.88\%), which places it in the favorable region. Other settings yield SC above 55\%, where confirmation bias dominates. The hyperparameters themselves ($K$, $T$) are not the causal driver; they modulate SC level, and it is the SC level that determines whether SICA has operating room. This reframes the sensitivity results: SICA is not sensitive to $K$ or $T$ per se, but to the SC regime that these parameters happen to induce.
70	
```

</details>

          " ",
          " Temperature sensitivity on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$, seed 42) reveals a sharp isolated peak rather than a smooth trend. Across four temperature settings, only $T = 0.7$ produces a positive effect: $T = 0.3$ yields $\\Delta = -1.47$\\,pp (SC $= 58.33\\%$), $T = 0.5$ yields $\\Delta = -0.98$\\,pp (SC $= 58.33\\%$), $T = 0.7$ yields $\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp (SC $= 55.88\\%$), and $T = 1.0$ yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp (SC $= 55.39\\%$). All other temperatures yield negative or null effects, and the single positive result at $T = 0.7$ is confounded with SC baseline: $T = 0.7$ produces the lowest SC (55.88\\% vs.\\ ${\\sim}$58\\% at $T \\in \\{0.3, 0.5\\}$). The temperature effect and the SC-regime effect cannot be disentangled from this data alone. Combined with the seed sensitivity (0 of 3 seeds reaching $p < 0.05$ at $T = 0.7$), the positive trend depends on a specific combination of temperature, seed, and baseline accuracy that same-model sampling rarely achieves, and even then does not reach conventional significance.",
          " ",
          "-The parallel between $K$ and $T$ sensitivity points to a common underlying variable. In both sweeps, the setting that produces the largest SICA gain also produces a relatively low SC baseline (55.88\\%), which places it in the favorable region. Other settings yield SC above 55\\%, where confirmation bias dominates. The hyperparameters themselves ($K$, $T$) are not the causal driver; they modulate SC level, and it is the SC level that determines whether SICA has operating room. This reframes the sensitivity results: SICA is not sensitive to $K$ or $T$ per se, but to the SC regime that these parameters happen to induce.",
          "+The parallel between $K$ and $T$ sensitivity points to a common underlying variable. _source line 7050 · 2026-05-19T11:20:08.424Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UWyJhwdfdcEdXnwoCWosbz`</summary>

```text
99	Two patterns emerge. First, low-entropy problems produce $\Delta \approx 0$\,pp across all eight conditions: when traces agree, constraints carry no information beyond the majority vote. This confirms that SICA reduces to SC by construction when trace consensus is strong.
100	
101	Second, the direction of SICA's effect on high-entropy problems depends on the accuracy regime, not on entropy alone. In the favorable region (Mistral-7B on FOLIO, seeds 42 and 123), high-entropy $\Delta$ reaches $+12.8$ and $+9.1$\,pp: traces genuinely disagree, and constraints from correct-answer traces provide discriminative signal that resolves the disagreement. In Type~A conditions (Qwen2.5-14B on FOLIO, $\Delta = -8.2$\,pp), high-entropy problems represent rare deviations from a strong model's consensus; constraints extracted from these problems inherit the model's confirmation bias and actively push toward the wrong answer. In Type~C (Mistral-7B on ProofWriter, $\Delta = -4.9$\,pp), high-entropy problems reflect systematic errors, and constraints formalize incorrect reasoning.
102	
103	The mechanism is not entropy per se but the interaction of entropy with model capability. In the favorable region, high entropy reflects genuine uncertainty where the model has partial but not dominant knowledge; constraints can tip the balance. Outside this region, high entropy signals either rare failures (Type~A) or pervasive confusion (Type~C), and constraints amplify rather than correct. The aggregate $\Delta$ is determined by the proportion of high-entropy problems and the sign of their contribution, which explains why the effect is regime-dependent (\S\ref{sec:main-results}).
104	
105	\subsection{Constraint Ablation}
106	\label{sec:ablation}
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe evaluated Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) across six models spanning three architecture families across three reasoning domains (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA), and over 3,000 evaluation instances with four-seed replication on the primary condition. Grounded in the Condorcet Jury Theorem, our analysis explains why Self-Consistency majority voting saturates and why symbolic constraint aggregation fails to break this plateau under same-model self-extraction.\n\nThe central finding is the independence bottleneck. Same-model traces produce Fleiss' $\\kappa$ values of $0.21$--$0.82$ among instruction-tuned models (0.91 for thinking mode), and the effective sample size compresses to Eff-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ at $K = 12$. Self-extracted FOL constraints inherit this correlation structure. We observe a narrow favorable region around 50--55\\% SC accuracy in one model-dataset condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO): Mistral-7B on FOLIO shows a positive directional trend of $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$, 2 of 4 seeds marginal at $p < 0.10$, none reaching $p < 0.05$; 4-seed mean $+1.47$\\,pp). Outside this zone, confirmation bias renders constraints non-discriminative (Type~A) or error amplification actively degrades accuracy by $-2.83$\\,pp (Type~C).\n\nOracle controls confirm that the aggregation mechanism is sound: gold FOL annotations yield $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling. Nine remediation strategies spanning six intervention dimensions all fail to widen the effective zone. The uniform failure confirms the bottleneck is structural rather than an engineering artifact. Sensitivity analysis reinforces this conclusion: $K = 12$ is the only setting with $\\Delta > 2$\\,pp among five $K$ values, and only 1 of 4 temperature settings ($T = 0.7$) produces a positive effect; both peaks coincide with a relatively low SC baseline.\n\nThe multi-candidate rate $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost deployment diagnostic for predicting when symbolic aggregation can help. Three paths forward trade simplicity for independence as CJT requires: trained extractors, external provers, and multi-model pipelines.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} Our evaluation covers same-model self-extraction and cross-model extraction in both directions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}), but we do not test trained FOL extractors or external solvers with verified grounding, which may overcome the independence bottleneck. The observed favorable region is narrow (SC $\\approx$ 50--55\\%), observed in only one model-dataset condition, limiting practical applicability. The optimal $K$ and temperature settings cannot be determined without labeled data, as both sensitivity analyses show the best configuration coincides with the lowest SC baseline rather than following a predictable trend. Our evaluation covers formal reasoning benchmarks (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA) but lacks open-domain validation where formalizability is lower and failure modes may differ. Finally, four-seed replication on the primary condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO) shows effects ranging from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp, with 2 of 4 seeds yielding null or negative results, indicating that even the most favorable condition produces unreliable improvements.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DvrdFSaRTggjpmNs5785Q9`</summary>

```text
11	\paragraph{Limitations.} Our evaluation covers same-model self-extraction and cross-model extraction in both directions (\S\ref{sec:remediation}), but we do not test trained FOL extractors or external solvers with verified grounding, which may overcome the independence bottleneck. The observed favorable region is narrow (SC $\approx$ 50--55\%), observed in only one model-dataset condition, limiting practical applicability. The optimal $K$ and temperature settings cannot be determined without labeled data, as both sensitivity analyses show the best configuration coincides with the lowest SC baseline rather than following a predictable trend. Our evaluation covers formal reasoning benchmarks (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA) but lacks open-domain validation where formalizability is lower and failure modes may differ. Finally, four-seed replication on the primary condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO) shows effects ranging from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\,pp, with 2 of 4 seeds yielding null or negative results, indicating that even the most favorable condition produces unreliable improvements.
```

</details>

    "newString": "The intermediate-SC region where positive trends occur is narrow (SC $\\approx$ 50--55\\%) and observed in only one model-dataset condition, limiting practical applicability.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe evaluated Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) across six models spanning three architecture families across three reasoning domains (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA), and over 3,000 evaluation instances with four-seed replication on the primary condition. Grounded in the Condorcet Jury Theorem, our analysis explains why Self-Consistency majority voting saturates and why symbolic constraint aggregation fails to break this plateau under same-model self-extraction.\n\nThe central finding is the independence bottleneck. Same-model traces produce Fleiss' $\\kappa$ values of $0.21$--$0.82$ among instruction-tuned models (0.91 for thinking mode), and the effective sample size compresses to Eff-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ at $K = 12$. Self-extracted FOL constraints inherit this correlation structure. We observe an intermediate-SC region around 50--55\\% accuracy in one model-dataset condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO): Mistral-7B on FOLIO shows a positive directional trend of $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$, 2 of 4 seeds marginal at $p < 0.10$, none reaching $p < 0.05$; 4-seed mean $+1.47$\\,pp). Outside this zone, confirmation bias renders constraints non-discriminative (Type~A) or error amplification actively degrades accuracy by $-2.83$\\,pp (Type~C).\n\nOracle controls confirm that the aggregation mechanism is sound: gold FOL annotations yield $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling. Nine remediation strategies spanning six intervention dimensions all fail to widen the effective zone. The uniform failure confirms the bottleneck is structural rather than an engineering artifact. Sensitivity analysis reinforces this conclusion: $K = 12$ is the only setting with $\\Delta > 2$\\,pp among five $K$ values, and only 1 of 4 temperature settings ($T = 0.7$) produces a positive effect; both peaks coincide with a relatively low SC baseline.\n\nThe multi-candidate rate $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost deployment diagnostic for predicting when symbolic aggregation can help. Three paths forward trade simplicity for independence as CJT requires: trained extractors, external provers, and multi-model pipelines.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} Our evaluation covers same-model self-extraction and cross-model extraction in both directions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}), but we do not test trained FOL extractors or external solvers with verified grounding, which may overcome the independence bottleneck. The observed favorable region is narrow (SC $\\approx$ 50--55\\%), observed in only one model-dataset condition, limiting practical applicability. The optimal $K$ and temperature settings cannot be determined without labeled data, as both sensitivity analyses show the best configuration coincides with the lowest SC baseline rather than following a predictable trend. Our evaluation covers formal reasoning benchmarks (FOLIO, ProofWriter, LogiQA) but lacks open-domain validation where formalizability is lower and failure modes may differ. Finally, four-seed replication on the primary condition (Mistral-7B on FOLIO) shows effects ranging from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp, with 2 of 4 seeds yielding null or negative results, indicating that even the most favorable condition produces unreliable improvements.\n",
